ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e149	The Family and Culture Committee has discussed Prop. 14 L on proposals for amendments to the Children's Act, etc. As the rapporteur, I would like to review the Committee's position and briefly outline the majority's views on the various legislative proposals. As a representative of the Christian People's Party, I will continuously account for our minority positions, where appropriate. Initially, I would like to stress that it is very difficult to regulate such a complex and emotional matter as the place of residence and association of parents after a break in life. It is important to maintain that it is essential to provide a legal basis for ensuring the safety and stability of each child in everyday life and to ensure, as far as possible, good contact with both parents. I am pleased that a joint committee stresses that it is a goal that, despite a breakup, children can maintain and develop good contact with both their parents. The Committee further points out that the ability of parents to cooperate after a break in life is of great importance to the child’s care situation. I am also particularly pleased that an overall committee demonstrates that the best interests of the child should primarily contribute to the child's perspective, and that this is the overall consideration of the wishes and rights of each parent. Furthermore, the majority of the Committee considers that, with regard to matters of residence and association, equality between parents is only relevant when it contributes to optimising the best interests of the child. The majority further point to the need for any legislative action to be taken in these areas in the light of the best interests of the child. The government has proposed legislative changes in five areas, which are currently being addressed by the Storting. There is a ban on all forms of violence against children, the obligation to provide warnings when moving with children domestically, divided residence, the definition of normal association and consent to health care. The majority support the Government's proposal to clarify Section 30 of the Children's Act. There shall be no doubt that children shall not be subjected to any form of violence. Furthermore, the majority of the Committee supports the Government's proposal to introduce a six-week alert obligation. The aim is to clarify the responsibility of parents to keep each other mutually informed about decisions which must be presumed to have a major impact on the life of the child. In the case of divided residence, it is worth clarifying that it means that the parents have the same authority over the child. It often also means, but not necessarily, that the child lives equally with each parent. Today, parents may agree to a split residence, but the courts cannot judge this. The Government has proposed a right to judge it before the courts when there are particular reasons. I'm counting on the majority here to explain his vision. The Christian People's Party will vote against the amendment proposal in Section 36. I refer to the report Shared residence for children, from the Institute for Social Research, which concludes that shared residence will function poorly in those cases where parents have a high level of conflict, because shared residence requires close cooperation. High levels of conflict between parents are burdensome for children. In the cases raised in the courts, the level of conflict will normally be high, and the conditions for extensive cooperation at the best of the child from day to day will not be present. The law’s requirement that shared residence must be based on a joint decision between parents also has a positive effect on the possibility of achieving an amicable settlement through mediation. In addition, the government is proposing an extension of the so-called standard of normal association. The Committee supports this. However, the Christian People's Party is moving towards that part of the extension of the norm that includes overnight stays in the middle of the week. The community code will be used in all types of life crimes. It is used in both cases of low conflict and in cases of high conflict. Overnight stays in the middle of the week require a higher level of parental cooperation on the follow-up to kindergarten, school and activities. It will vary from case to case the degree to which such cooperation and follow-up assumptions are present. I take up the Christian People's Party proposal at this point. In order to avoid that one of the parents prevents the child from receiving the health care it needs, the Government of the Proposition proposes a change in the Patient Rights Act so that it is sufficient for one of the parents to consent to health care when health professionals consider that the child may be harmed by not receiving health care. The Committee supports this.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e181	The representative of Øyvind Håbreek has taken up the proposal he mentioned.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e184	Breakup is one of the most dramatic things that can hit a child. Therefore, the Children’s Act is important in order to protect children’s interests both in terms of life crimes and in other areas. The proposals for changes to the Children’s Act presented today by the Government come as a consequence of our having a more equal parenthood, and that both parents take care of and care for the children to a far greater extent than before, and that fathers have a much more central role in family life than they had in the past. Still, it's like that in 80 pst. of the cases following a divorce, the children live with their mother. But we see that there is a development in which parents in several cases choose a shared-dwelling solution, and in those cases you choose it, it has happened through an agreement between mother and father without it being in either court or other place. We must have legislation that is as neutral as possible and that does not discriminate against one of the parents. This is why, among other things: put forward a proposal by the Government on the issue of relocation, which now introduces a six-week alert requirement if the parent wishes to move. But it also applies to consensual parents. This means that with a six-week alert, you have the opportunity to surround yourself and talk about whether it is natural for the child to still live with the resident parent – if that is the best. You have the opportunity to discuss whether it's a good solution, what is now being chosen. You also have the opportunity to ask for mediation if you can't agree, and in the worst case, if you can't agree on mediation, you have the opportunity to solve this relationship through court. What is important is that moving is a rather dramatic decision for both the child and for those of the parents who have the right to cohabit, because it can mean getting less contact with the parent, and the child gets less contact with his or her father or mother. That's why it's very important that we get this alert now, and the committee has great reason to believe that it will have a good effect. So the case's rapporteur mentioned the issue of association and that the government opens up to change the legal definition, to expand it. One aspect of which there is disagreement in the Committee is the question of extending with one overnight stay in the week. All the other parties support the Government's proposals, with the exception of the Christian People's Party. We believe that it is completely unproblematic to expand with one overnight stay this week, because we believe this is in line with the practice of very many families today, and we believe that the law thus adapts to the developments we see in society now, where fathers increasingly want to have close and close contact with their children. The other relationship on which there is disagreement within the Committee is the right of the courts to judge divided residence. The government is now opening up for the courts to have access to it. Once again, all the other parties support it, with the exception of the Christian People's Party. The Children’s Law Committee, which was set up by the Government, recommended that access to this should be open to the courts. They thought it was fundamentally objectionable to reconstruct the jurisdiction of the courts as it is today. And that is what the majority of the committee agrees with. Nevertheless, we would point out that only in exceptional cases will it be relevant for the courts to judge divided residence, because when the case comes to court, it is so intense and the conflict between mother and father is so intense that in most cases it will not be relevant to judge divided residence because the cooperation will be so poor. But there may be some reason why it would actually be best for the child to judge divided residence, and we believe that one should not exclude those cases and exclude the possibility for the courts to do so. Another aspect, which is on the side of this proposal, but which I think may have the same effect, is that one gives a signal from the legislator to society that more equal treatment is being given to parents, and we believe that when one opens the way for the court to judge divided residence, it will actually help fewer cases end up in court, because the parents feel equal.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e247	This bill affects the daily well-being of very many people. Unfortunately, very many families — by and large half — experience this, namely a break in their lives. It is not an abnormal situation or a strange situation, it is a difficult situation, but a situation in which we, as legislators, have a great responsibility to find good management. For us as legislators, the main goals are clear. We must help to ensure that children in families where a break-in occurs have the best possible living conditions and have the opportunity to maintain and develop contact with both father and mother. The best of children is below all. That's the guide for us, and we're going to make sure that the laws take care of that main point. One of the most important questions in the Government’s proposal is whether or not to allow the courts to judge a divided residence. The right has previously been skeptical of such an opening. The background for this is, as has been mentioned here before, the fact that in cases where this comes up, there will be a high level of conflict, and the possibilities for the child to come into a very difficult situation are great. The second argument is that in a practical life enough, there is a need to exert some pressure on the parties in family matters to actually come up with reconciliation and find good solutions, as the families themselves are the closest to seeing what can be. When we have come to the conclusion that we will support the Government's proposals, it is because we see that general legislation makes it difficult to take care of all possible individual situations. In these cases, the court will be in a difficult position, but the court will be able to deal with the individual case in a completely different way from what we can do, and in this way we should rely on the legal system's ability to maintain the child's best and find good solutions – or at least the best possible solutions – also in these situations. The right-hand side also supports the correction of the community norm so that it covers one more night a week. Like the Government, we believe that this is a correction that, based on what most of today's parents would want, is a natural development. The right also supports the ban on all forms of violence against children. We believe such a rule is a sign of health and a proper signal in a democratic and non-violent society. All forms of violence rekindle violence and brutality. Unfortunately, we live in a time and in a society where violence is increasing, and all the distance from this is in the Right's view a good thing. The main conclusion for us is that we support the proposals presented.

That is why I am pleased that we are changing this law to strengthen the legal certainty of children, and that the Children's Ombudsman supports the amendments. The law does not ensure protection alone, but gives clear signals to all parents about what are acceptable forms of education in Norway. It's not allowed to beat children, it's not allowed to slap children, it's not allowed to lock children in a room, it's not allowed to threaten children to be abandoned or injured, nor is it allowed to demean and humiliate children. That is the most important thing we are adopting today.

I believe that we could have been allowed to use coercion against adults also in the Children's Act, because coercion is evil against children, and it makes it difficult to exercise the role of both parents. We therefore believe that there should be no coercion against children when children have not done anything wrong, but we believe that it should be an opening to use coercion against adults. That doesn't mean you're arresting someone, but that means, for example, that you can hold someone back from work because that day someone else is going to pick up at the kindergarten. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e310	About 20,000 children experience a yearly separation of parents, and for most children this is a good thing, although the break between parents can be a great impression for some children. In addition, it is clarified that children should also not be subjected to daunting or abusive behaviour or any other negligent behaviour. With such a clarification of the legal situation, we send out a very clear signal to all parents about what are acceptable recruitment methods in Norway and what is not. I therefore welcome the broad support and am disappointed that the support is not unanimous. Of course, a change in the law is not sufficient to prevent violence against children. From the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and the Ministry of Inclusion, we have created a guide on cooperation between kindergarten and child protection to help child care workers follow up their concerns about a child. I am pleased that the Committee supports an alert obligation before moving to apply to both parents – both the residence parent and the cohabiting parent. It will give parents time and opportunity to discuss and assess what will be the best care solution for the child in the future, before moving. I note that the majority of the committee agrees that the courts should have a narrower access to judge the divided residence against the will of one parent or both parents. The minority in the Committee – Christian People’s Party – believes that the prerequisites for necessary cooperation for the best interests of the child will not be present once the case has been brought to justice, because the level of conflict in these cases must be assumed to be high. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e517	At the request of the Committee on Family Affairs and Culture, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 40 minutes and allocated by up to 5 minutes to each party and up to 5 minutes to the Council of State. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e526	This is a matter that has created a lot of commitment among many, and that's not so strange. The right to freedom of belief and the right to protection against discrimination are both fundamental human rights. Freedom of faith implies a right, both as an individual and as a religious community, to exercise their faith in line with the doctrine of the faith society. At the same time, protection against discrimination is another fundamental right. Individuals are entitled to equal treatment and not discrimination on grounds of sex, race, ethnicity, religion, age, sexual orientation and function. Then it is necessary to grant both of these rights. But since they can conflict with each other, we have to find a good balance. The majority of the Committee considers that the draft law meets the needs of religious communities for a core of freedom of religion and autonomy through continued freedom of exception from an absolute prohibition of discrimination. Today, there are, in particular, rules in the Equality Act and the Labour Environment Act that regulate access to discrimination between women and homosexuals by religious communities. It is proposed to abolish the specific access of religious communities to discrimination based on homophilic forms of life. At the same time, it is important to maintain a certain freedom of exception for religious communities to discriminate on grounds of sexual orientation or sex. The general rule of procedure is sufficient in relation to access which religious communities should have in order to discriminate against applicants for sexual orientation. It means that discrimination must be factual, necessary and proportionate in view of the discrimination suffered. It in turn reinforces the requirements for justification for the discrimination of religious communities. It is desirable to make as clear a provision as possible in this area, and the majority therefore support the draft law repealing the provisions on the freedom of exemption for religious communities, as it provides a vague indication of the positions covered by this derogation. The majority support the view that discrimination against women should be seen in the context of the proposed access to discrimination due to the homofil form of life. The majority support the Government's proposal to amend Section 2 of the Gender Equality Act, which means removing the reference to «with the exception of the internal conditions of religious communities» so that the law will apply in all areas of society. In addition to proposals for changes to the Labour Environment Act and the Gender Equality Act, it is proposed to strengthen the protection of pregnant women. It is proposed to legislate a ban on asking about pregnancy, adoption and family planning in an employment process. This will help to strengthen the already legal protection of discrimination, and it was supported by an overall committee, which I think is very positive. Although this information cannot be emphasised today, it is natural that we now have a ban on asking for it. These are demanding issues where it is important to strike a good balance between different rights we have in society. I believe that in this case we have found a good balance between different fundamental human rights. Otherwise, the majority of the Committee supports the Government's proposal for amendments to this matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e541	Following the entry into force of the Joint Marriage Law a year and a half ago, the specific access of the Labour Environment Act to discriminate against homosexuals in the employment of religious communities remained the only area of Norwegian legislation that allowed discrimination against homosexuals. Already in the Soria Moria I agreement, government parties concluded that it was necessary to rectify this by investigating the non-compliance with the Equality Act and the Working Environment Act. The Graver Committee was set up in June 2007 and, in its first interim inquiry, the sample concluded that both the prohibition of discrimination and the freedom of religion in international human rights conventions must be taken into account in the design of the Norwegian law. The committee stated that discrimination must be justified by the religious teachings of religious communities. The employment of religious leaders — priests, teachers in the religious community — lies at the heart of the provision. On the other hand, the selection was more uncertain as to whether other positions in businesses run by religious faith communities could be protected by religious freedom. The proposal promoted by the Government found a balance between the two human rights which clearly conflict with each other, namely the protection of discrimination and freedom of religion. The government’s proposal means that the special exemption for religious faith communities is abolished. Recruitment requirements for employment in jobs with duties related to the exercise of faith or religious activity are instead covered by the general rule of procedure in the first paragraph of Section 13-3 of the Working Environment Act. Discrimination on the grounds of sexual orientation must be religiously justified and necessary from the point of view of the religious community or the religious teachings of the enterprise. The employment of religious teachers, priests and teachers in religious communities is at the heart of the provision. But the Government also proposes a legal framework that provides conditions for discrimination to be met in matters relating to other types of positions that have a religious function in religious communities and their activities. For example, there will be posts in kindergartens and schools where workers participate in religious education. The legislation we have today opens up a general access for religious communities to discriminate against homosexuals. It violates the provisions on discrimination in international human rights law, and it was also pointed out very clearly by the Graver Committee. The proposed text of the law therefore restricts access to discrimination and does not allow for more discrimination than is necessary in the interests of the autonomy of the religious community as regards the content of its religious teachings. Another problem with today’s legislation is that it is vague in wording, and the scope of the paragraph is also very much debated. I therefore find it quite strange that the opposition will keep a law that is so unclear, and in many ways so debated, in relation to the interpretation. But I understand why the Christian People's Party and the Advancement Party will give religious communities the freedom to discriminate against homosexuals. What I do not understand, on the other hand, is why the Right supports the Advancement Party and the Christian People's Party in this matter. The right has previously appeared to be a hot defender of the gay people's rights, but now it unfortunately looks as if the gay people in the Right have lost in relation to the more religious forces there.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e558	In particular, changes in the Working Environment Act have been the focus of this matter, and with good reason. The proposal for amendments here is a clear restriction on religious freedom, as the Progress Party sees it at least. In fact, I'm going to say that the Government does its own interpretations, or has its own understanding of what is religion, and what religion means to people – and to schools and religious communities. In almost all countries around the world, at least those we want to compare with, religious freedom is strong. Even though the government is trying everything it can intensify it, they admit that one has to have certain exceptions. Now they're going to tighten up further on these exceptions. This conflicts with freedom of religion. Today's bill is such an intensification and in such a direction that the Progress Party will not support this. The right to freedom of belief is a fundamental human right. Yes, the freedom of employment here must be seen in the context of the fact that religious communities themselves must be allowed to determine their internal lives. Troll-based establishments must and must be able to define what is best for them without interference from the State. Schools, boarding schools, employees — perhaps laundry workers, office staff or night shifts, etc. – are, in fact, important for the individual religious community. Individual schools will make slightly different demands on the positions. The basics of each activity and the content of the positions are defined by the enterprise itself, without interference from us politicians. Those who run free schools or colleges don't do this to get as close as possible to the public school. This is also about parents' right to choose education for their children according to their own religious or philosophical beliefs. The progress party is afraid that the substantive requirement that the government is now putting up with is so unclear that we are going to have more cases in the courts in the future. Is this what we want? The government parties are very much talking about tolerance and diversity. This law will lead to more uniformity and less diversity, and the Government will then, with such a policy, go in the wrong direction, as the Progress Party sees it. We are seeing again that the Government's eagerness in such matters is at the expense of religious freedom. Finally, for the Progress Party, this has been a perfectly clear case to vote against. But I have to say that I was about to put the coffee in my throat when I discovered that the Christian People's Party in the media appeared a little vague and would negotiate with the Government in this matter. If there was one thing I knew the government wasn't going to four in, it was probably just this one. Fortunately, the Christian People's Party discovered it in the last hour. As the legislative power of the State, the Stortinget owes it to protect fundamental human rights such as freedom of belief and freedom of organisation. The Progress Party believes that today's legislative proposals weaken these rights and cannot help supporting these intrusions into the Working Environment Act.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e620	Modern, liberal democracy is characterised by the need to guarantee fundamental rights for all people. No matter who you are, where you come from, your skin color, lay, sex, cultural background or religious affiliation, the state must guarantee your rights and ensure you against discrimination. For us as a legislative authority, it will always be a primary task to ensure that the law is good enough all the way to ensure these fundamental rights for all people. This includes, inter alia: the responsibility for further developing the law to prevent discrimination. There is also the background to this proposition being considered today. Firstly, there is no good contribution to combating discrimination against homosexuals, and secondly, it weakens the position of religious freedom in this part of our law. The right to protection against discrimination cannot give any of us the right to be employed in faith-based businesses where this would be contrary to how the religion in question – based on its own teachings – believes it is right to operate. There is no evidence to suggest that the first paragraph of the same paragraph provides for access to discrimination on a factual basis. Therefore, it is a brief conclusion when the Government refers to general access to discrimination on a factual basis and refers the country’s faithful people – almost to whether they believe in one or the other – to reading the precursors of the law to see if their human rights are respected. Of course, these rights belong in the legal text. That's actually why we have a law. It's got to be the majority's responsibility to explain. Nevertheless, when the government parties would do so, the religious and life-vision organisations made clear to the Committee on the understanding of religious freedom that should have been the basis for interpreting so-called factual purposes and practice. In a letter to the committee dated 3 February, they say, among other things: the following: ‘As representatives of religious communities and faith-based businesses in Norway, we will encourage the Stortingen to base the right religious communities and other religious establishments to define for themselves what positions are related to the purpose of their activities and the requirements of loyalty and lifestyle that must therefore be imposed.’» The letter is signed by presidents of the Norwegian Church and the chairperson of the Cooperation Council for Religious and Lifely Societies, which otherwise represents the Human-Ethical Union in the Council. The Christian Council of Norway, the Islamic Council and several other organizations are also behind the letter. In the setting, the government parties have not joined this recommendation. I find it alarming that when Stortinget deals with an important issue about the fundamental rights of minorities and the breadth of religious and life-view organisations gather with a common claim to the legislator, the government chooses to firmly reject it. In a true democracy, the majority power should never be used to restrict the fundamental rights of the minority. So we have a government in this country that can use its majority to sovereignly reject the minority's request to have its fundamental rights respected. So, even if we have a matter of freedom of faith to deal with, the government clearly says no to the Norwegian Christian Council and the Norwegian Church, they say no to the Islamic Council, they say no to the Human-Ethical Union – because we have a majority, we have to define their rights. It's arrogant. But worse than that, it's not in the deal of democratic ideals in a modern, liberal society. Finally, I would like to say that it is quite revealing when the representative Gjul links this to the opposition's different voice in the issue of marriage law – and even performs to say that it is the religious forces in the Right that have won over the homophobic. More clearly, it cannot be illustrated that for the red-greens, this case is concerned with how they – their perception, the majority’s view – define the rights of minorities, and that is to turn the case upside down.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e658	The protection of discrimination in Norwegian law is a central instrument for the fight against discrimination and for the realisation of human rights. Figures from the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman show that discrimination against pregnant women in Norway is a problem. More than one third of the applications to the Ombudsman in the field of employment are about discrimination against women. We have therefore proposed that a ban on pregnancy be introduced. By imposing such a ban, we are at the same time making it even clearer that pregnancy cannot or must be stressed in the event of employment. The proposal will clarify the legal situation in this area and help to protect women from this type of discrimination. The same considerations also apply to parents who are to adopt and questions of family planning. A ban in the law will strengthen the protection of discrimination in this area. I am very pleased that the Committee agrees with the proposal to strengthen the protection of pregnant women from discrimination. I repeat that I am proud that the Government is taking this initiative and that the Storting supports it, but at the same time I must also say that I am ashamed that this is necessary for employers in Norway in 2010. I hope they listen to the signal that Stortinget is giving today. The right of individuals to religious practice and the right of individuals to protection against discrimination are fundamental elements of a democratic society and from a human rights perspective. Human rights are indivisible and cannot be negotiated. The right of individuals to freedom of religion is protected by several human rights conventions and gives the right to exercise their faith alone or with others. Religious freedom therefore also includes freedom for religious communities to, for example, determining the teachings and practices of the religious community. Religious communities are also employers, and in this context there is a need to clarify the relationship between the protection of individuals from discrimination and the autonomy of the religious community in relation to the teaching and practice of employment. I am pleased that the majority of the committee supports the Government's proposal to repeal the special rule in the Working Environment Act § 13-3, third paragraph. This implies that religious communities must meet the same requirements as other employers in relation to discrimination. That is to say, discrimination must be objective. It must be necessary and proportionate in relation to the subject of discrimination. This in turn means that religious communities must be able to justify their desire to discriminate on the basis of sexual orientation or sex. I want to strengthen the protection of individuals against discrimination – including within religious communities. I take note that the members of the Committee of the Progressive Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party – and now clearly also a united bourgeois opposition – believe that the proposal for amendments to the Working Environment Act significantly tightens the ability of the religious communities to actually establish their activities in relation to their own doctrinal beliefs, and that they believe that the Government's proposals narrow down the freedom of religion. But I repeat: freedom of religion is a right for the individual, while freedom of religion gives faith societies autonomy in relation to the practices and teachings of the religious community. In the case of religious societies such as employers, it has been necessary to clarify the relationship between the autonomy of religious communities and the individual's requirements for protection against discrimination. I want that protection strengthened also within religious communities. That's this balance we've been trying to figure out with these changes in laws. I believe – as opposed to the minority in the Committee – that the amendment of the law will provide clearer leadership and more manageable conditions. In the event of discrimination, the general rules of procedure lay down clear conditions to be met by the religious communities. They are described in detail in the legislative proposal. I also refer to the Government's proposal to change the employer's access to information about the jobseeker and the Committee's support for that proposal. With regard to the ability of religious communities to discriminate on a gender basis, this is regulated by the Equality Act. Religious communities will only be able to discriminate on the grounds of sex in relation to religious functions, rituals and employment if it is factual, necessary and proportionate. I believe that the legislative changes in the Equality Act and the Working Environment Act will lead to a reinforced protection against discrimination. There will be clearer rules on when discrimination is allowed, and I think that will benefit all parties.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e849	In the last decades, Norway has become a more and more secular society. We've become a society that's also become more and more liberal. Personally, I think that's a good development. I appreciate living in a society where there's more room for being different, where there's more room for having an equation different from that of the majority -- all of these things. At the same time, I think you see trends that the pendulum has turned in the opposite direction, that we get some sort of illiberal liberality in society that forces us to ask the question: do we have room for the religious at all? Do we have room for those who still think that religion is important, that faith is important, and want to live their lives and build their institutions around it? Illiberal liberality is basically a well-meaning concept. It's about my liberalism going to apply to everyone else as well. So the question is, isn't it in a society that there should be limits to what politicians should be able to impose on liberalism? Our task is to secure some fundamental rights for everyone – yes. But the task of Storting is also to recognise where the limits of politics should go – that there are some people and some institutions governed by slightly different principles in some areas than perhaps the large, liberal majority will do. There's a balance sheet, and I think this law goes too far. The question is in the ultimate sense: Who is to decide? Some common frameworks and some common rules should be in place. But in religious societies it must be that theology is the basis, not the opinion of the Council of State. We've had a tradition in Norway because we've seen the Norwegian Church as a kind of under-use of the state. We have had a tradition that several parties have been afraid of the Church and how the Church should affect the State. One should perhaps also be afraid of how the state should influence the Church. There are some difficult dilemmas. Equally, I think we're going to have a society where there's room for faith, space for religion, and where religious communities are going to be allowed to rule themselves without too much interference from the Council of State, even if the Council of State had to have good and liberal intentions.

The government has agreed to submit an individual right proposal to BPA, user-driven personal assistance. This mechanism has been important to promote independence and independence for people with disabilities. Children, too, must be given individual rights to this service, from birth onward. This could help to increase the freedom of choice for families, including: because they may choose to have assistance at home so that the child can live at home. The Christian People's Party believes that the Government needs to submit legislative proposals as soon as possible to ensure individual rights to user-led personal assistance. In the debate there are several who have said that one does not want a jump and jump method where legislative proposals or important arrangements are adopted right above the table. But I would like to remind you that this proposal was submitted a long time ago, and similar proposals have been adopted before, and the government has announced that a wish to come back with matters. It should not have been in time in a case of so many good actions, which, to the extent that the government parties speak positively both in the vote and in the debate. For the Christian People's Party, it has been important to be able to promote a measure to just meet our goal, that everyone should be allowed to live as they wish. Finally, I would just like to present the proposals of the Christian People's Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e1072	I am pleased that the Stortinget draws attention to the situation of children and young people with disabilities and their families. Through Soria Moria II, the government has committed itself to working towards better and coordinated assistance for vulnerable children and young people and their families. Furthermore, it has been announced that the existing strategy plan for children and young people with disabilities will be reviewed. Support shall be provided for holiday and leisure schemes for children of low-income parents, and provision shall be made for the relief and assistance of families with children with high disabilities. These are commitments that we have already made well. It is also signaled that the system of user-led personal assistance should be extended. In recent years, the government has launched several measures and processes that will help improve the situation of families with disabled children. I would like to mention, in particular, the establishment of learning and mastering centres in hospitals, which have developed offers for families with children and young people with disabilities. A separate national website, family network.no, is an important tool in this context. From 1 January this year, the Directorate for Children, Youth and Family has been responsible for the continuation and further development of this website. In 2007, the Family Wizard project was established. It is a further development of the so-called TaKT programme, where the focus was on the organisation of municipal services and the promotion of competences in the municipalities of the country. The Family Guide project has now established models for a more comprehensive and coordinated service provision for children with disabilities. These models shall ensure real user involvement, individual level and primary responsible service provider to families who so wish. The project focuses on interaction between the different levels of administration. I recently received an investigation into better coordination of services for vulnerable children and young people from the so-called Flatø Committee. I intend to send this investigation in broad consultation during this month. The follow-up must also be seen in the context of the Mid-Lineg Committee setting, which focuses on the situation of students with special needs. Relevant is also the follow-up to the Interaction Reform and work on new common health and social services laws, where coordination of services also has a central place. And I very much acknowledge the importance of raising this issue. On 1 January 2009, the new Discrimination and Availability Act entered into force. This is a law that is at the forefront of the Nordic region in its area and provides protection against discrimination because of disability. The basis for discrimination includes both physical, mental and cognitive disabilities. The law imposes an obligation of both general organisation and individual organisation. Infringement of these rules of organisation is regarded as discrimination. The law contributes to ensuring equal opportunities and rights for children with disabilities for training, education, equivalent health and social services and social inclusion. The law also protects parents from being discriminated against because of their child’s disability. I would also like to point out that the Government has presented a very offensive action plan for universal design and increased accessibility, which will work positively for all age groups. We also follow the Law on advice or other representation arrangements in municipalities and county municipalities for people with disabilities etc. These advices allow for increased power and security of performance in local work. In cooperation with other departments, we will finish work on reviewing the current strategy plan for children with disabilities and their families and assess the need for further action. We want to stress that children with disabilities and their superiors have sex. Girls and boys, women and men can face different challenges in different life situations. Measures targeting disabled children and their families must take this into account.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e1223	The size will now deal with the National Audit Office’s study of the financial management of our healthcare companies. The Committee has conducted open consultations on the matter with the Trade Union, the Norwegian Confederation of Nurses, the Norwegian Medical Association, the Directors of the regional health companies, the former Minister of Health and Care Sylvia Brustad and the current Minister of Health and Care Anne-Grete Ström-Erichsen. The opinion shows that there is a great deal of agreement in the Committee on the matter, and only in a few comments is the shared view. I'm assuming that the parties here are briefing on their views. I think it is worth stressing that the Riksrevision has carried out a comprehensive and thorough investigation, which provides both a good basis for the Storting to assess the situation and gives a good indication of where the effort needs to be directed in order to improve governance. The national audit has highlighted the issues by means of interviews, document analysis and a questionnaire for health companies. In order to examine the financial management of health establishments, written documentation of relevant procedures and guidelines has also been obtained. During the period 2002–08, the operating costs of health establishments increased sharply. The total operating costs of regional health enterprises increased from SEK 57 billion in 2002 to DKK 87 billion in 2008. Some of the growth in operating costs is due to the fact that regional health enterprises have been given new tasks, but taking into account the new tasks, there has nevertheless been an annual real growth in operating costs of between 1 and 4 pst. during this period. In 2009, more than DKK 100 billion were allocated to the specialist health service. The Committee considers that the National Audit Office’s study identifies a number of shortcomings in the management and control of the use of resources in healthcare companies, and in view of this, those actors in health care establishments were invited to open consultation. Regional health enterprises had an accumulated deficit of DKK 25 billion between 2002 and 2008. The deviation from the management requirements of the owners was 700 million in 2008. NOK, and then after the Storting had granted 500 million. kr. An overall committee states in its opinion that it considers it unacceptable that, year after year, health businesses have failed to adapt to the financial framework. The Committee also notes that expectations of additional appropriations have contributed to undermining respect for the budgetary process and thereby hampering the transition. The employees’ organisations are now expressing that such expectations are gone and that such conditions will not affect further operations. The National Audit Office’s study shows that many health establishments do not start the budgetary process early enough to identify, plan and implement conversion measures in order to give effect from the beginning of the financial year. In this context, the Committee notes that during the Committee’s consultation, representatives of staff are calling for better anchoring at all levels, better predictability and long-termity in the budgetary process. The Committee has noted that both regional health establishments and health establishments wish to improve the predictability and long-term of the owner and that late dispatch of the mission document is a major cause of non-coordination. On the part of the government parties, we are pleased that this is a matter that has been addressed, and we assume that good solutions are being found in this context. An overall committee has noted that the financial management of health establishments has improved significantly in recent years and that the forecast is positive in the future. The Committee is not aware of the accounts for 2009 but notes that representatives of the board of directors at the hearing said that the 2009 forecasts show that Health Norway is on an overall financial balance and thus meets the owner’s financial performance targets.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e1325	The accumulated accounting deficit of health enterprises for the period 2002-2008 was DKK 25 billion. The majority of this was run by the owner. Many health entities delegated budgetary responsibilities to managers at the level where the costs were generated, despite the fact that more than half of the same managers thought they had neither competence nor sufficient capacity to manage the economy of the unit. Fortunately, things indicate that evolution is now moving in the right direction. Back in 2007 and 2008, Health Mid-Norway was the only healthcare company that satisfied the owners’ financial management requirements. The 2009 accounts have not been completed, but the forecast shows that Health-Norway together is in a balance sheet and meets the requirement. Differences between health enterprises are also significantly reduced. According to Kolbjørn Almlid, Head of Health Mid-Norway, better budgetary processes, better involvement at all levels and a greater focus on internal control and cost control. The centre party opposed the reform that led to the governmentisation of hospitals. The strong growth of health businesses in operating costs from 2002 to 2008, from SEK 57 billion to DKK 87 billion, suggests that the intention, a more reasonable health care service with more health for each krona, has not in any way been successful. Health bureaucracy has grown, and lack of continuity in governance has been a contributing factor in the lack of financial management. The fact that the management salaries of health enterprises have grown in line with the accumulated deficit is worrying. Are we getting a directorial field in the Norwegian health care system where the closure of surgical wards and maternity wards takes place completely without political treatment? The centre party believes that the business model needs to be evaluated and that hospitals need to be transferred to a regionally selected ownership. We hope that the visit of the State Council of Ström-Erichsen to Scotland, a country that abolished the business model in 2004 and now introduces direct elections to regional health authorities, can serve as an inspiration for Norway in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e1583	The size has set the objective of ensuring that the operation and maintenance of the road network contribute to the safe and secure availability of road users throughout the year and that this must be carried out with the correct quality and timeliness. In the Riksrevision report, Document No. 3:16 for 2008-2009, which saw the operation and maintenance of the road network in 2008, a number of shortcomings have been identified in relation to this objective. The report identifies a lack of quality of operation, a lack of reporting on the state of operation and a lack of management and an overview of the operational status of the road network. Furthermore, the Riksaudition has identified a lack of evaluation of an area of governance challenges, a low level of control and poor follow-up in many contracts, different practices of financial penalties, poor reporting by entrepreneurs, weaknesses in the evaluation of entrepreneurs as well as weak competition among providers in many places. In the light of this massive criticism, the Control and Constitutional Committee decided to hold a hearing on the matter on 8 February this year, inviting both the former and the current Minister for Transport and Road Director. In addition, the Committee subscribed to a number of user organisations and invited them to make written submissions in the case. The Committee received input from Norway's Lastebileier Association, the Swedish Transport Association, the Norwegian Motorcycle Union, the Norwegian Automobile Association, the Cyclists National Association, the Car Action and the Road Traffic Information Council. User organisations largely adhered to the criticism made in the National Audit Office’s report. Some recommended that, in addition, a much wider and more thorough study be carried out even in the case of regular maintenance. There was also concern from several organisations that the so-called regional reform could lead to major differences in the operation and maintenance of the road network as well as further pulverisation of responsibility. Several of the Committee's recordings also called for the establishment of independent roadside supervision, which must be responsible for ensuring the safety and accessibility of road users, and for monitoring the proper follow-up of the intentions of the legislation and policy decisions. In addition, some pointed out a lack of competition in functional contracts and called for a fragmentation of the contracts. I am pleased that the Minister for Transport would consider this as one of several measures to strengthen competition. The cost of using functional contracts has, of course, been a topic during the Committee's proceedings, and this was also the focus of the consultation. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e1648	In 2003, a major shift was made in the state road system, and maintenance tasks previously carried out by the Road Administration were gradually subject to competition through so-called day-to-day operating and minor maintenance contracts. The centre party was skeptical about the competitive outsourcing of the State Road Service when it was implemented. The National Audit Office’s report shows that the objective of the reform, reduced costs, has not been achieved in any way. On the contrary, expenditure has increased at the same time as road maintenance standards have fallen. Expenditure went down somewhat in the first round of tenders, but rose dramatically when the contracts were renewed. Contracts renewed in 2009 received a price increase of 44 pst. At the same time, it was necessary to provide greater resources on the construction side. With over 100 functional contracts on 5000 pages each, it goes without saying that there was a need for resources to get in place and not least to follow up the contracts properly. An evaluation of the 2007-2008 season shows a lack of work carried out for more than half of the contracts. The national audit also highlights the lack of reporting on the state of operation of the road network, the low level of control and the lack of follow-up. This does not ensure that the road system meets road users’ expectations for good and safe accessibility throughout the year and does not provide sufficient management information. The first contracts to be concluded were mainly fixed-price contracts. More recent contracts now present more risks to the country in terms of quantities. I think that will have a positive impact particularly on winter maintenance, as it will no longer be as profitable to wait for the longest, for example. by breaking. As of 1 January, the county has been responsible for a much larger part of the road network, which means greater financial liabilities in the functional contracts. The Road Director explained good cooperation with the county councils in advance of the regional reform. The centre party believes that a common road management system for county roads and highways, better localisation and greater proximity to decision-makers will provide both better quality road maintenance and better resource use. The solution may not be to reverse this entire competitive outsourcing process now, which would create new uncertainty for employees around the country. The focus on the short term must be primarily on making the right adjustments to get the best out of the situation. Maybe a break-up of the large functional contracts for several smaller and more transparent contracts would be a way to go. It would also allow smaller local entrepreneurs with good local knowledge to participate in the bidding rounds. Too many bidding rounds have been marked by the fact that there have been only a few large companies that have been on the offer page. Perhaps smaller contracts will also lead to increased competition, and in this way a measure to improve the functioning of today's maintenance model. The Ministry of Transport has engaged Dovre Group AS to evaluate the competitive outsourcing of operations and maintenance in the State Road Administration. The final report shall be submitted at the spring part. That means travellers on long-distance trains get 50 pst. discount on the ticket price if the train is more than 60 minutes late. For local trains, the scheme applies after 30 minutes. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e2566	The system of organised transport, often referred to as the TT system, is entirely decisive for people who cannot use the ordinary collective service. It's about facilitating an inclusive society for everyone, a society where each individual can use their resources for the benefit of themselves and others. It's about human dignity. The Christian People's Party is very concerned that the TT system should work as well as possible. Full participation and equal opportunities for people with disabilities are an important policy objective with broad support in this room. The quality and scale of the transport service is crucial to our achievement of that objective. I know that the Minister for Transport is busy with the rights of the disabled. In the debate on the State budget before Christmas, I experienced a constructive Council of State in relation to the TT regime. With this interpellation I want to take the matter one step further. We have to put in place a TT system that guarantees their disabled people the right to participate in society in the same way as everyone else. There is a need for a national system of door-to-door transport for disabled people with a high need for such a service, a transport system that gives the number of journeys that take place in order to live a normal and active life. The basis for a political solution is good. A majority of the representatives in this room have answered a clear and clear yes to direct questions about whether they would be in favour of a national system of door-to-door transport for the disabled. 87 of the representatives have shown their commitment and have declared themselves to be active and personal support players. Storing representatives from all parties have joined this goal. It is difficult to see a better starting point for a good solution in TT case. We now have a responsibility to get this into port, after disabled people's organisations have been fighting for a better transport system for 30 years. Variations in different parts of the country are large and random. Most counties offer 2 to 4 individual trips per month. In comparison, the study of travel habits to the Institute of Transport Economics shows that people generally have three daily trips on average. In the most active periods of life, people travel much more. After going back and forth to the store, many people with disabilities have used a fourteen-day transport quota. The parent meeting, the daughter's football training or a movie tour won't fit. I've heard a lot of personal stories. A woman for 40 years from Skåk has transportation for NOK 1,054 per person. years. For her this keeps to four tours at the store during the year. She has nothing left for other activities or personal activities. Her husband must always run kids and follow up his children’s activities. A man from Gausdal in his 30s has a transport charge of NOK 2000 per person. years. It covers four tours to Lillehammer annual. The situation, of course, goes beyond the mere possibility of being active enough. A 50-year-old woman has $2,400 TT a year. Because of large distances in the municipality, this only lasts for a trip to the municipality's center of the year. The TT situation leads to social isolation for many people. They rarely get out of the world, they don't get to visit friends and families, they can't attend meetings or organisations, and the situation is beyond the ability to follow their own children. The blindfold and disabled people's organisations have made good proposals to the Transport Committee on how a national TT scheme for people with disabilities with high needs can look like and how it can be organised. This should not be a special scheme that gives special privileges to a selected group, but a system that ensures the same possibilities for the disabled as for all others. Therefore, the link between the property and the property is underlined. TT-tours should be set at the same level as collective tariffs and, in the case of longer trips, TT-travel should, where possible, be combined with public transport. The TT scheme was a disability scheme from the very beginning. However, a large proportion of TT users today are not really disabled, but they have had problems made up as a result of old age. 70 pst. Today, users are over 70 years of age and about 10 pst. is under 60 years of age. Studies show that the oldest user group is mainly satisfied with today's offer. That's good. A new scheme should not go beyond these. Users who are not heavily dependent on the scheme should still receive a good TT offer from the group in the future as well. A new national regime should apply to those people who, due to disability, are critically dependent on the transport system in order to live a normal and active life. This user group is limited in size. Estimates estimate that a satisfactory provision of door-to-door transport for disabled people with high needs will cost about 200 million. kr. Liability for such a national scheme may be added to Nav. This is what one chooses to do for the schemes of work and education travel. The offer can be given, either as a given number of trips per. the user, or the user may receive a payment card with a certain amount of kroner. For both of these options, the state already has developed technical solutions. Payment cards must be usable all over the country. A system of ordering transport has been developed in individual contexts as a possible solution to the transport situation of disabled persons. However, ordering transport offers little flexibility and is a model that does not work for user groups with high needs. One does not come out if you want or need it, and it may take a very long time to get to the place you want. If you are going to pick up the train, an event, a dental session or follow your children on symmetry, the transportation of orders is not working. Active visual impairments depend on the same flexibility as everyone else to make everyday life go up. In the budget debate in Stortinget on 10 December, the Minister for Transport said that she, like me, was busy improving the TT system. The Council of State showed that there have been many honors for a long time, and that we have not been able to follow up well enough in this matter. My question to the Council of State is: Will the Council of State ensure that the disabled with much need are equipped with a TT system that allows for a normal and active everyday life – no matter where in the country a cage? In his speech in December, the Council of State itself pointed to what will be the decision as to whether we will have a good order in place. It's about the seventh and last thing about counting on the state budget. The Christian People's Party will give priority to TT-saka. We want a solution that allows disabled people to live active and equal lives. In order to retain the necessary lessons and experience, a test scheme can be started in at least two selected states. I also hope that the other parties, the Government and the Council of State will do their job to get this in place, for example. a testing scheme that will provide the necessary experience to improve the national TT system.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e2606	Better organisation of transport for disabled people is a high priority area for the Government. The concept of universal design has become an integral part of the work and priorities of the Ministry of Transport and State Transport. Both laws, regulations and physical measures financed by the State budget have made the public transport system more accessible under this government. With effect from 1 January 2009, the rules of the new Equality and Discrimination Act were applied. New rules for the design of buses and passenger boats have been introduced. New rules for the design of trains are soon in place. Universal design and accessibility are also one of the main objectives of transport policy, presented in the National Transport Plan 2010–2019. Infrastructure and means of transport shall be designed in such a way that collective transport solutions can be used as much as possible without resorting to specific means or assistance. But the area has been neglected for a long time, and the challenges are great. In addition, many people with disabilities will depend on alternatives to public transport, regardless of the quality of collective provision. For many, organized transport, the TT system, is decisive for the ability to live an active life, and it is very important that this system works well. The government's strategy to secure the disabled with a high need for a good TT system is two-fold. Firstly, we want to secure the county councils, which are responsible for both the ordinary local collective provision and the TT system, an economy that enables them to ensure both a good TT supply and a good collective offer. In my opinion, this is the most important thing that can be done to ensure good mobility for all, including TT users. Transfers to the municipal sector have increased sharply during this government. The county councils have gained a significantly larger economic space and far better opportunities both to ensure a good collective offer and to provide a good TT offer to those who are unable to benefit from ordinary public transport. Secondly, we will help to ensure that local collective services are used by the most mobile TT users, so that resources can be freed and used for a better TT system for users with the most needs. This can be achieved by better physical design of the public transport system. The commitment to universal design both through equality and discrimination laws and through state budget promises is important. In addition, the Government has stimulated testing of different operational concepts, including: through the better public transport system in the districts, where the counties can apply for assistance, inter alia: to test different forms of demand-driven transport, e.g. order transport and service routes open to all. These are schemes where users report their travel needs to a transport centre or directly to the bus, boat or taxi operator within a specified time limit and where there is coordination of the individual journeys. Such schemes may provide better use of resources than individual TT schemes. For some TT users, this type of supply is probably entirely acceptable. For children, young people, the elderly and other people without either TT card, driver’s card or access to the car, the provision of order transport is decisive. Its more likely to benefit from ordinary collective services, the more liberal the TT regime can be designed for those users who need this scheme the most. In order to investigate better coordination between TT supply and on-demand transport, the need for TT transport can be reduced without compromising the mobility of users, the Ministry of Transport in 2009 invited the municipalities to participate in a three-year trial where the two offers are seen explicitly in context. Seven counties applied to participate, and there are now attempts to improve coordination between collective provision and TT provision in the Oslo municipality and six counties, Østfold, Telemark, Vest-Agder, Rogaland, Sogn and Fjordane and Nord-Trøndelag. In all trials, the order transport must be expanded. At the same time, adaptations shall be made to the TT service legislation to ensure that most TT transports are carried out with the regular public transport services, including order transport and service routes open to all. User involvement is required of disabled people's organisations, and TT supply is required to be maintained by teams for those who cannot benefit from the expanded transportation service. The aim of the experiment is to see whether such an organisation will release resources that the county authorities can use to strengthen the provision of TT users with special needs, whether the majority of travel for other users can be covered by the extended provision of the transport of orders, and whether the offer increases the mobility of users outside the TT scheme. The ministry will carry out a thorough evaluation of the experiment after three years. So a few words about national guidelines for the TT scheme. It was in the Community economicproposition for 2004 that the Stortinget took a decision asking the Bondevik II Government to develop national guidelines for the TT scheme. This question has been carefully considered. There are guidance guidelines for the TT scheme, developed by the Ministry of Transport, where the criterion for user authorisation, the content of the offer and the procedure itself are mentioned. But in the same way as local public transport, the county councils are the ones who set the standard of supply. The guidance guidelines mainly form the basis for the guidelines applicable to the TT regime in the state. It is then that national guidelines will provide a greater level of non-discrimination than the budget-driven system we currently have under the authority of the county. Challenges may be linked to a system of national guidelines. National guidelines may lead to monthly additional costs, if one is not to accept a poor supply for some users. In addition, national guidelines will break with the established division of responsibilities between the levels of administration and the principle that the size and quality of the provision of public transport are to be determined by the county authorities. National guidelines will reduce the possibilities for municipalities to see TT transport in conjunction with other public transport. As I just did, a solid county economy, combined with a collective offering that can be used by the most, will be a good strategy to ensure a good TT supply for those users who need it the most. The interpellant takes up a very important question here. I have also invited several organisations to the Ministry to ask for advice on possible improvements in the TT regime. The last organisation I met was also the Blind Alliance. I understand their concerns over the county councils and their free incomes with regard to whether funds that may be released are really being used to strengthen the TT regime for those who need it most. The blindfold was creative. They asked for another attempt, one such as could include two counties where a direct link to a TT scheme for those who are unable to benefit from collective provision. I can't promise at this point that I'm going to have additional attempts in the 2011 budget, but what I'm going to say is that I'm busy actually strengthening the TT system. So this proposal and other constructive contributions are something that I think there's every reason to look at.

If you put in place a good order or service route, it will catch up with many of the older users who really just need a good public transport. They can use an order route, so you get a lot of those in the TT service today, on that arrangement. Then you are left with a door-to-door transport system for those who cannot use this system either. I believe that the binding to public transport can then be loosened, not at price, but at availability, because then we are in an area where the transport needs are different from others, because there are people who have no choice, and – to put it that way – most of them cannot drive a car, and they cannot use public transport. It's an important task for us to make sure that they can live an equal life, because that's what it's all about. Then there are questions about national guidelines. I think that public transport is a municipal responsibility. The part of this is about ordering routes or service routes, is county responsibility. But I think that if we're going to put in place a system that ensures the door-to-door transport for those who need it, we have to look at some ways to ensure this, with greater equality across the country than what we have today. I think that the principles of equal transport opportunities for all are more important than the division of tasks that we once agreed on. My experience is that if there are different arrangements across the country with a very large amount of discretion, it works very well for groups who are able to exercise great political power and fight and fight for their rights, while those who have problems with it, simply because they may not be able to transport themselves where the decisions are taken, are much more dependent on the law-making of national policies and security in the schemes. Then they are able to be equal and to uphold their democratic rights.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e2920	At all times, the ocean giant, the whale, has attracted human attention. Norwegian whaling is of very old date. Evidence has been found that coastal people in Norway have already in ancient times taken feeding from smaller whales. The heavy tax that had taken place only in the 1930s and previous years led to a sharp decrease in the stock of whales. This led to certain limitations. After World War II, it became clear around the world that if the whaling was to be intensified again, the catch had to be regulated so that the whale population would not be destroyed. The International Whaling Commission was therefore established in 1949. The capture of seals and whales is a traditional industry in Norwegian seas. A responsible withdrawal of marine mammals may also have beneficial effects on the total exploitation of other marine resources. At the same time, marine mammals are icons with high symbol values for nature and animal protection. It is therefore very important that Norway has a balanced management of these species and that management is based on a sound scientific basis, professional advice and the widest possible international acceptance. Traditionally, coastline, stone copper and garden have been a desirable resource along our coast. As a result, local populations were almost eradicated as a result of heavy hunting pressure. The interest in coastal settlement declined after World War II. The result was a certain increase in the population of stone copper and yard along the coast, which in turn has led to that, among other things. the fishing industry wishes to reduce the number of coastlines. The authorities wish to mitigate any conflict between coastal zones and fisheries by stimulating increased hunting in order to regulate the stock, while at the same time wanting to preserve viable coastal populations in the natural areas of selenium. There is a need for increased knowledge of coastal and fisheries conflicts, including the role of the coastline in the coastal ecosystem. In Norway, the coastal zone is a source of strong negative reactions. Locally, the coastline can be perceived as a problem and a scourge with consequences for quality of life and professional pursuit. The damage caused by the coastline to fisheries may be grouped into three categories: damage to implements, selenium consumption of commercial fish species, spreading of mercury to fish For those who don't know what horny is: it's a nasty parasite, or a roundworm, that sits in the fillet of the fish. It helps to reduce the quality of the fish by reducing the suitability of the fish for fresh placing on the market or as frozen fish. It is claimed at regular intervals that there are more seals along the coast than researchers can detect. In 1987 we had an invasion of Greenland'ssel along the coast of Finnmark. I am pleased that the communication shows that, in the event of a future selinvation, the Government will use funds to limit the damage and impact on the industry along the coast. In addition, the fisheries and aquaculture industry is invited to cooperate with the researchers in concreteising the damage caused by the coastline to the fisheries and aquaculture industry and on methods of measuring the extent and extent of these damage. I am pleased that the committee's recommendation is promoted by an overall committee, and that it is cross-politically in line with the policy of marine mammals that the Government is pursuing. This message is available to many in the industry who have also been waiting for it. The work of this government on the policy of marine mammals is mainly based on two pillars: to carry out serious scientific work in accordance with international law; Norway follows its obligations under international law to international cooperation in the research and management of whales and seals. The overall objective of the government is to harvest it sustainably from all the marine resources, including cell populations in good condition. Norwegian self-anxiety is subject to strict and detailed legislation covering fishing seasons, quotas, methods of killing, self-rescue training, vessel approval and control. Each year, the catching people must undergo training and fire tests before they go to catch. All the shooters have inspectors on board. The inspectors are veterinarians — or equivalent — and report directly to the fisheries authorities. That's the way it should be for the future. Driving with self-anguish doesn't make you rich. The financial support provided by the State is aimed at facilitating a profitable industry by providing support for product development, redistribution and catch efficiency measures. The results of Norwegian self-afraid in the last five years show that the industry is still unprofitable and dependent on public support. The market situation for whales and fur products is challenging, but it must also be said that the demand for such products is limited. In the case of whiting meat, Norway and Japan are the only market of importance. In the case of sales products, in particular sales and sales oil, the market has already been affected by the EU’s import ban on cell products, with declining demand and a fall in the price of leather. In the case of skins and oils, the industry is now oriented towards Asia — and it is exciting — to look for new markets. It is based on this Communication that the measures and policies otherwise addressed in this Communication will not trigger new financial burdens to any extent. Nor has this Communication promoted measures that could be expected to lead to any degree of administrative impact. The Government will strengthen and further develop the fishing industry in order to contribute to ensuring sustainable business profitability. There's a lot to say for the districts. Taking care of the fishing culture along the Norwegian coast is also an important element of this policy. If it succeeds in achieving better profitability in the fishing industry, it will have a cyclical impact on the coastal communities in which the fishing industry is established.
