ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-09.ana.ud1954e3272	The Bondevik II Government presented St.meld in 2004. No 27 for 2003–2004 on Norwegian marine mammals policy. The Communication gave a broad review of the policy area and launched a series of measures that the government wanted to pursue in order to achieve its policy objectives. The communication was widely welcomed by the Storting and provided the basis for long-term and holistic approaches to the management of marine mammals. During the examination of the notification, it was decided that the government should return to Stortinget with a new notification after a certain period of time. St.meld. No 46 for 2008-2009, Norwegian marine mammals policy, is a follow-up to that Decision. The content of the Communication explains the follow-up to the measures launched in St.meld. No 27, as well as the other decisions taken by the Stortinget in its examination of the notification. Harvesting of whales and seals represents on a Norwegian scale small industries with few prisoners and relatively small processing and distribution activities on the land side. This, for its part, represents specific challenges for the authorities to facilitate maximum profitability and efficiency in the performance of activities. International conditions particularly characterise the framework conditions of the marine mammals industry. The situation in the International Whaling Commission and the EU decision to stop imports and placing on the market of cell products are examples of processes that challenge management and industry in a particularly resource-intensive way. At the same time, Norwegian marine mammals policy is subject to some strong criticism from a number of individual countries. International criticism is at the heart of Norway’s right to self-determination in relation to the sustainable use of our natural resources. The government's position on that point is clear: as long as there is a scientific basis for harvesting sustainable populations, other, political considerations will not prevent us from doing so. Overall, national and international challenges call for a broad range of instruments in some demanding interactions. It is therefore important to ensure cross-political integration of marine mammals also through a separate communication to the Stortinget. St.meld. No 46 for 2008-2009 states that it is this government’s desire to stick to the broad lines to which the Storting joined in 2004 and to take further action in order, in particular, to strengthen the knowledge base and ensure the long-term and sustainable management of our marine mammals. At the International Whaling Commission's annual meeting in 2009, we had to admit that the work on getting the organisation into more functional forms had not worked. The IWC's problems have a history that stretches back decades and has its roots in a strong polarization between catchland on one side and catch opponents on the other. The conclusions of the 2009 annual meeting therefore came – unfortunately I must say – not as a surprise. At the same time, there was a strong willingness among Member States not to give up the experiment and to recognise that the work started should continue until the 2010 annual meeting. The position of Norway in the IWC is clear. We stand on a safe footing in international law and in science, and our whaling is hardly disputed on such a case-by-case basis. Norway may set the quotas for which there is a scientific basis and as permitted by a responsible resource management. For Norway, in other words, the situation in the IWC is not an administrative problem. At the same time, there is a clear need for international cooperation in research and management related to whaling, such as for fishing and catching of the living marine resources. As a starting point, the Government continues to have an ambition to contribute to the normalisation of the IWC. The government wants to be quite clear on that. It's IWC as per. current date is the International Forum for Cooperation in the Research and Management of Whaling. However, to what extent we will reach the goal of normalising the organisation remains uncertain. The uncertainty about the future of the IWC as a management organisation was an important justification for Norway’s participation in the establishment of the North Atlantic Maritime Animal Commission in 1992 — NAMMCO. As a responsible resource manager, we wanted to make sure that we had a regional cooperation organisation in place in case the IWC would collapse. This relationship with NAMMCO also applies to this government, while NAMMCO has strengthened its role as management organisation for small whales and seals since 1992. The catch quota is set on the basis of an allowance calculation model approved by the Scientific Committee of the IWC. For 2010, the Government has set a wave whale quota of 1,286 animals, 885 of which are the base quota and 401 have been transferred as the unused quota from last year. In the last year's season, 484 wavy whales were caught. Whaling products are mainly traded in Norway and the industry is responsible, both on the sea and on the land, for most of the quota being taken and distributed on the market. Whaling traps and shore-based reception units need to work together to improve their overall effectiveness and to increase the strength of the whale meat in the Norwegian market. Globally, Japan is the only market that can receive whale meat of importance. Since it was decided to re-open the market for the export of wafer products in 2001, we have therefore worked specifically to open up the Japanese market. An agreement with Japan for the export of whale meat was put in place in 2007. The agreement was negotiated with Iceland. The first export lot of Norwegian and Icelandic whale meat was sent in May 2008. While the Icelandic product batches entered, Norwegians were rejected due to excessive levels of lactic acid bacteria. We have from our side arranged for the regular export of wafer products to Japan, but Japanese health and sanitary requirements must be respected in order for the batch of products to be approved for import. The Norwegian whale industry must be actively involved in the treatment of the whale meat intended for export to Japan. Here, the authorities are constantly having good dialogue with the industry. So, for the self-anxiety industry. The Committee's recommendation suggests that it should be harvested sustainably from all marine resources, including cell populations in good condition. The government will continue to facilitate a sustainable harvest of sales based on scientific advice. Cells form a natural part of the marine ecosystem and are an integral part of the multi-population management of our marine resources. We must therefore continue to work to ensure self-reliance, export and product development. Self-employment is a small industry, but it has long traditions in Norway. The industry has over time experienced significant economic challenges. This has helped to get shots active today. Substantial public support related to the self-anxiety activity itself has proved to be a necessary tool for maintaining the industry. For 2010, I have decided to support self-anxiety in the West ice within an upper framework of 7.5 million. kr. Maximum grant per year vessels will depend on the number of vessels going. If they want operational alternatives in addition to self-gang, they must initially obtain them within the framework of the rules in force. The industry is also challenged internationally. The EU has adopted an import ban on cell products. It is a clear attack on self-anxiety and Norwegian resource management. Even before the ban has entered into force, we have seen the effects on the market for cell products, with lower turnover and lower prices for leather. The government believes that the ban violates the EU’s international trade obligations. As has been mentioned here this evening, we have held dispute resolution consultations with the EU, and we will consider taking the matter to a WTO panel. In this context, compliance with the Norwegian Self-Anxiety Code is essential. The authorities have done a great deal of work to obtain acceptance for Norwegian fishing. If it turns out that there is a breach of the law that weakens confidence in Norwegian self-anxiety, we are very serious about it. Therefore, such cases are followed up by the responsible authorities. We live up to our obligations to ensure the long-term, comprehensive and sustainable management of our seas. The government wants to maintain the prevalence of coastline and ensure viable populations within their natural distribution areas. Coastal stock growth shall be regulated within this framework in order to remedy damage to the fishing industry and other interested interests. The government is working on long-term management strategies for coastal populations. The work on the strategies is in a final phase and will be completed after the Storting has addressed this Communication.
